Time periodic solutions for the 2D Euler equation near Taylor-Couette flow
Ángel Castro and Daniel Lear

Abstract
. In this paper we consider the incompressible 2D Euler equation in an annular domain with non-penetration boundary condition. In this setting, we prove the existence of a family of non-trivially smooth time-periodic solutions at an arbitrarily small distance from the stationary Taylor-Couette flow in H s H^{s} , with s < 3 / 2 s<\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}} , at the vorticity level.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
76E07,76B03,35Q31,35Q35

1.
Introduction and main result

1.1.
Motivation and background

The 2D Euler equation is an old but still very active field of research. Unlike its 3D version, the global existence of smooth solutions is a classic result dating back to the 1930’s, see
[
63
,
85
]
. The qualitative behavior of these global smooth solutions is a very important aspect of the study of Euler flows. However, their long time dynamics is difficult to understand in general due to the lack of a global relaxation mechanism.

An important class of flows, in terms of long term behavior, are those that are independent of time.
Then, a more realistic task is to study the dynamics of solutions near steady-states.
Coherent structures, such as shear flows, radial flows and vortices, are especially important in the study of the 2D Euler equation, since physical experiments, numerical simulations and formal asymptotic show that they tend to form dynamically and become the dominant part of the solution at very long times.

The stability of these steady-states is a classical subject and a fundamental problem in the context of
hydrodynamic stability
and has been considered by many authors. We refer to
[
7
,
37
,
66
]
for a general overview of this topic and a more detailed list of references.

1.2.
The Taylor-Couette flow

In this article we are interested in the 2D Euler dynamics near Taylor-Couette flow in an annular domain.
This radial flow is given by

(1)

u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(r)=Ar+\frac{B}{r},\qquad r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}.

Here
A,B\in\mathbb{R}
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,\infty)
are the inner and outer radius of the annular domain.

Such type of two-dimensional flow can be obtained experimentally by rotating two concentric cylinders, where the rotation is slow enough not to cause a 3D Taylor-Couette instability.

Despite being a simple type of steady solution, perturbation of Taylor-Couette has proven to be a challenging topic and has been extensively studied experimentally, theoretically and numerically for a long time. Many questions remain unanswered, making it an active field of research in fluid mechanics, see
[
23
,
46
,
62
,
79
]
.

Now, for the sake of completeness, we recall that velocity, vorticity and stream function associated to the stationary Taylor-Couette
flow are given in Cartesian coordinates by

{\bf u}_{\textsf{TC}}({\bf x})=\left(A|{\bf x}|+\frac{B}{|{\bf x}|}\right)\frac{{\bf x}^{\perp}}{|{\bf x}|},\qquad\omega_{\textsf{TC}}({\bf x})=2A,\qquad\psi_{\textsf{TC}}({\bf x})=\frac{A}{2}(r_{1}^{2}-|{\bf x}|^{2})+B\log\left(\frac{r_{1}}{|{\bf x}|}\right).

In order to present our result, we start considering the vorticity formulation for the Euler equation in the two-dimensional annular domain
\overline{B_{r_{2}}(0)}\setminus B_{r_{1}}(0)
with no-penetration boundary conditions, i.e.
{\bf u}\cdot{\bf n}=0
on
\partial B_{r_{2}}(0)\cup\partial B_{r_{1}}(0)
. Then, we have

(2)

\partial_{t}\omega+{\bf u}\cdot\nabla\omega=0,\qquad{\bf u}=\nabla^{\perp}\psi,

with the stream function
\psi
satisfying
\Delta\psi=\omega
and the boundary conditions

(3)

\psi|_{\partial B_{r_{1}}(0)}=0,\qquad\psi|_{\partial B_{r_{2}}(0)}=\gamma.

Clearly,
\psi
is defined uniquely by
{\bf u}
up to a constant. Therefore, without loss of generality we set
\psi|_{\partial B_{r_{1}}(0)}=0
. The constant
\gamma
may in general depend on time but by Kelvin’s theorem on conservation of circulation
{\bf u}
is also independent of time along the flow. In fact, see (
226
) we have

\gamma=\frac{-1}{2\pi}\int_{[r_{1},r_{2}]\times\mathbb{T}}u_{0}^{\theta}(r,\theta)\,\mbox{d}r\mbox{d}\theta.

Since we are interested in the 2D Euler dynamics near Taylor-Couette, we will set the circulation to be exactly the same as the Taylor-Couette flow, i.e.

(4)

\gamma:=-\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(r)\,\mbox{d}r=-\left(\frac{A}{2}(r_{2}^{2}-r_{1}^{2})+B\log\left(\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right)\right).

1.3.
Main result

The 2D Euler equation can be seen as a Hamiltonian system, and thus it is quite natural from a dynamical system point of view to explore whether time periodic solutions near specific equilibrium states may exist.

For the sake of clarity we shall now give an elementary statement of our main result. Let
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty
and
A,B\in\mathbb{R}
with
B\neq 0
, then the following result holds:

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
0\leq s<\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}
and
\epsilon>0
, the 2D Euler system (
2
)-(
4
) admits a non-trivial smooth time periodic solution
\omega
with frequency
\lambda
such that:

•

The distance to Taylor-Couette flow satisfies that

\|\omega-2A\|_{H^{s}(\overline{B_{r_{2}}(0)}\setminus B_{r_{1}}(0))}\leq\epsilon.

•

The frequency
\lambda
of the temporal periodicity is of order
O(1)
with respect to
\epsilon
.

Remark 1.2
.

Non-trivial means solutions that depend on the angular variable in a non-trivial way.

This result will follow from Theorems
10.1
,
10.2
and
10.4
, in which the properties of these solutions can be further elaborated.

1.4.
Steady states and their (asymptotic) stability

The problem of finding steady states solutions for 2D Euler has been addressed by Nadirashvili in
[
77
]
, where he studies the geometry and the stability of solutions, following the works of Arnold

[
3
,
4
,
5
]
.
In recent years, a large number of results taking into account the geometry of the steady states and their flexibility/rigidity properties have emerged, see
[
19
,
20
,
22
,
24
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
67
,
74
,
78
]
and references therein.

There are a multitude of steady solutions of the 2D Euler equation. For example, every shear flow in a strip-type domain or every radial flow in a circular-type domain. Being a steady state imposes the condition that vorticity gradients are locally parallel to gradients of
the stream function
(\nabla^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla\omega=0)
.
So, as a consequence, a large class of steady solutions can be generated by specifying a Lipschitz function
F:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and solving the elliptic problem
\Delta\psi=F(\psi).

There is a privileged class of such steady state solutions given by solving the above elliptic problem, for some specific
F
, called
Arnold stable
. These flows are nonlinearly Lyapunov stable in the
L^{2}
topology of vorticity. It is also well known that there exist particular steady state solutions which possess even stronger stability properties like the Couette flow on the periodic channel which is nonlinearly Lyapunov stable in
L^{\infty}
.
For the most up-to-date overview, see
[
34
]
and references therein.

Asymptotic stability can also occur, although the theorems and scenarios are much more subtle and challenging.
This convergence back to equilibrium, despite time reversibility and the lack of dissipative mechanisms, is now known as
inviscid damping
and shares analogies with
Landau damping
in plasma physics, see the celebrated paper of Mohout-Villani
[
76
]
and references therein.

This work on
inviscid damping
, initiated with the breakthrough of Bedrossian-Masmoudi
[
6
]
, established that sufficiently small perturbations of the Couette flow in the Gevrey spaces
\mathcal{G}^{s}
, with
s>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, converge to a possibly new shear flow near Couette in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. The exact same result does not hold for
s<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, as it was shown in
[
30
]
by Deng-Masmoudi. See also the very recent results
[
18
,
86
]
for the inhomogeneous case.

In recent works Ionescu-Jia
[
64
,
65
]
and Masmoudi-Zhao
[
75
]
proved that nonlinear asymptotic stability holds true also for perturbations around the Couette flow and more general monotone shear flows in the finite periodic channel
\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]
.

For the steady state of interest in this manuscript, i.e., the Taylor-Couette flow it is important to mention the very recent results
[
1
]
and
[
2
]
, where the authors investigate the nonlinear stability of the Taylor-Couette for the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations.
See
[
9
,
21
,
38
,
87
]
for other related results on the mathematical study of the Taylor-Couette flow or more general circular flows.

Motivated by the results mentioned above, the linearized equations around more general shear flows were investigated intensely in the last few years, see for example
[
10
,
31
,
32
,
51
,
68
,
69
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
88
,
89
]
and their references for a complete, but not exhaustive, list of references.

An interesting related work is that of Lin-Zeng
[
71
]
, where they proved that nonlinear inviscid damping is not true for perturbations of the Couette flow in
H^{s}
with
s<\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}
. More specifically, they discovered Kelvin’s cat’s eyes steady states
nearby to the Couette shear flow. In fact, the dynamics at this low regularity is even richer and there are also travelling waves with velocity of order
O(1)
as showed by the authors in
[
17
]
.

Recently, there has been a growing interest in the study of existence or not of invariant structures, and their stability for 2D Euler near other shear flows and for related equations, see
[
22
,
36
,
70
,
72
,
73
,
78
,
80
,
81
]
.

1.5.
Tools and ideas behind the proof

An important tool for the proof will be the use of bifurcation theory, which has been very useful in showing the existence of solutions to various equations that arise in the field of fluid mechanics.

The fact that the vorticity of the solutions we are interested in is not constant within its support induces a more complex spectral problem due to the larger dimension of the space on which the linear part of the equation acts. This topic turns out to be less explored in the literature and only a few relevant results are known, see
[
16
,
42
,
45
]
.

To be more specific, we point out that the vorticity profile we are interested in is constant outside a pair of very thin regions
I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1}},I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{2}}
located at
R_{1},R_{2}\in(r_{1},r_{2})
where the dynamics occurs, and the thickness
\varepsilon
of this regions is related with the distance of the steady Taylor-Couette flow and the time periodic solution that we obtain in the procedure.

1.5.1.
Comparison and differences with Couette flow

Compared to shear-type Couette flow, the circular-type Taylor-Couette flow involves additional coefficients
A
and
B
that take into account rotational effects. Unlike the monotone and linear Couette flow, the appropriate choice of the free parameters
A\in\mathbb{R}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
makes the Taylor-Couette flow a much more interesting and challenging radial flow, which can have regions of concavity and convexity.

We observe that for the case
B=0
, i.e., constant angular velocity, the perturbations rotate while maintaining their shape at the linear level. However, in the case when
B\neq 0
, the perturbation is “sheared” in a way reminiscent of plane Couette flow.

An important difference with respect to the Couette flow is that here we have to be much more careful when choosing the parameters
R_{1},R_{2}\in(r_{1},r_{2}).
The choice of points
R_{1},R_{2}\in(r_{1},r_{2})
around where
\supp(\nabla\omega)
is concentrated depends strongly on the geometry of the Taylor-Couette flow profile. As we will see in Section
6
, in order to get a one dimensional kernel of the linear operator we need to impose that

R_{1},R_{2}\in(r_{1},r_{2})\quad\text{such that}\quad u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(R_{1})\neq u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(R_{2}).

Another important difficulty to be faced is related to the form of the Green’s function associated to the Laplacian in an annular domain. The situation in bounded domains turns out to be more delicate due to the presence of boundaries. This has an impact on the study of the regularity of the functional that will describe the periodic solutions over time.

For the Couette flow we used the explicit representation of the Green’s function of the Laplacian in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, which facilitates the study of the regularity of the functional. However, for the radial Taylor-Couette flow, as we are working in an annular domain, we do not have a manageable explicit expression for it. This type of difficulty has appeared previously in the literature. For example, in
[
61
]
, for the generalized surface quasi-geostrophic equation in the disc. In that paper, the authors circumvent this issue by a suitable splitting into a singular explicit part and a smooth implicit one induced by the boundary of the domain. That is,

K^{\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{c_{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}+K_{1}^{\alpha}(x,y),\qquad x,y\in\overline{B_{1}(0)},

where
K_{1}^{\alpha}
is a smooth function in
\overline{B_{1}(0)}\times\overline{B_{1}(0)}
.

In the present paper we use a different way to study the regularity of the
F
-functional introduced in (
18
), which can be easily adapted to other settings. Roughly speaking, we conduct a detailed study through the
elliptic problem associated to the stream function.

1.6.
Time periodic solutions in the literature

The topic of the existence of time periodic solutions is a classical issue in fluid dynamics, with a long history and many contributions.

In particular, following the approach of Burbea
[
11
]
, there has been many works concerning the existence of single or multiple patches and their degenerate case of point vortices not only for 2D Euler equation but also for other two-dimensional active scalar equations such as the generalized surface quasi-geostrophic equation through contour dynamics equations paired with bifurcation
theory, desingularization techniques and variational tools.
We refer to the interested reader, for more details, to the non-exhaustive list
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
26
,
28
,
29
,
39
,
41
,
47
,
49
,
50
,
55
,
56
,
60
,
59
]
and the references therein.

Very recently, some important progress has been done on the existence of time quasi-periodic solutions using KAM techniques and Nash-Moser scheme, see
[
8
,
48
,
57
,
58
]
and references therein.

1.7.
Notation and organization of the paper

Here, we will define the annular domain of interest using Cartesian and polar coordinates respectively. That is,

(5)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{r_{1},r_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\{{\bf x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:r_{1}\leq|{\bf x}|\leq r_{2}\}\equiv\overline{B_{r_{2}}(0)}\setminus B_{r_{1}}(0),

(6)

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\{(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}:r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}\}\equiv[r_{1},r_{2}]\times\mathbb{T}.

In the rest, we are going to work mainly with polar coordinates. Therefore, setting the parameters
\varepsilon>0
small enough and
R_{1},R_{2}\in(r_{1},r_{2})
, we define an auxiliary domain
D_{\varepsilon}
within
\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
where all the dynamics occurs. This domain
D_{\varepsilon}
will be given by

(7)

D_{\varepsilon}:=I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}\times\mathbb{T},

with

I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}=I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1}}\cup I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{2}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{i}}=(R_{i}-\varepsilon,R_{i}+\varepsilon).

1.7.1.
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we will write the equation for the vorticity contour lines via the Biot-Savart law. In Section
3
, we introduce the spaces we will work with to apply the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem and study the regularity of the nonlinear functional. To obtain a more manageable linear operator, we decompose and rescale it appropriately in the Sections
4
and
5
. In Sections
6
,
7
,
8
and
9
we conduct the spectral study of the linearized operator around zero and under suitable assumptions we obtain a Fredholm operator of zero index. Finally, in Section
10
we quantify the distance between our solution and the Taylor-Couette flow and address the complete regularity of the constructed time periodic solution.
In the Appendix
11
we recall and give the proof of some auxiliary lemmas used in the paper.

2.
Formulation of the problem

Since we are considering an annular domain, that is, a domain of circular type, it will be natural to work in the more appropriate setting of polar coordinates.

In polar coordinates
(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}
, the vorticity formulation for the Euler equation in the two-dimensional bounded smooth annular domain
\overline{B_{r_{2}}(0)}\setminus B_{r_{1}}(0)
with no-penetration boundary conditions, i.e.
{\bf u}\cdot{\bf n}=0
on
\partial B_{r_{2}}(0)\cup\partial B_{r_{1}}(0)
reads as

(8)

\partial_{t}\omega+u^{r}\partial_{r}\omega+\frac{u^{\theta}}{r}\partial_{\theta}\omega=0,\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},

where the velocity vector field
{\bf u}=(u^{r},u^{\theta})
is recovered from the vorticity
\omega
by means of the stream function
\psi
, via the relations

(9)

(u^{r},u^{\theta})=\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\psi,-\partial_{r}\psi\right),\qquad-\left(\partial_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta\theta}\right)\psi=\omega.

The main task of this section will be to obtain a functional equation whose solutions give rise to time-periodic rotating solutions of

(10)

\partial_{t}\omega+\frac{1}{r}\left(\partial_{\theta}\psi\partial_{r}\omega-\partial_{r}\psi\partial_{\theta}\omega\right)=0,\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},

with stream function
\psi
solving

(11)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{B}\psi(r,\theta)&=\omega(r,\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},\\
\psi|_{r=r_{1}}&=0,\\
\psi|_{r=r_{2}}&=\gamma.\end{aligned}\right.

Here and in the rest of the papaer,
\Delta_{B}\equiv-\left(\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\right)
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator and
\gamma
is the circulation given by (
4
).

2.1.
The 2D Euler as an equation for the level sets of the vorticity.

In order to find solutions of (
10
) by looking to the level sets of
\omega
. We assume that level sets of vorticity
\omega
can be parameterized by
\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)=(\rho+f(\rho,\theta,t))(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
inside the support of
\nabla\omega
, in such a way that

(12)

\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)=\varpi(\rho),\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon}\subsetneq\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},

for some smooth profile function
\varpi
.

We remark that, since the 2D Euler equation is a transport type equation, we can assume that profile function
\varpi
does not depend on
t
without any loss of generality.

Here, we assume that
f(\cdot,t)\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
for all
t\geq 0
with norm small enough,
\|f(t)\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\ll 1
. We immediately have that
\Phi_{t}(D_{\varepsilon})\subsetneq\mathsf{C}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
. This fact allows us to write (abusing the notation a little) the rest of the annular domain in Cartesian coordinates as follows

\mathsf{C}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\setminus\Phi_{t}(D_{\varepsilon})=\Omega_{\textsc{Inner}}(t)\cup\Omega_{\textsc{Middle}}(t)\cup\Omega_{\textsc{Outer}}(t),

with

\displaystyle\Omega_{\textsc{Inner}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\{(\rho,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}:r_{1}\leq\rho\leq R_{1}-\varepsilon+f(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta,t)\},

\displaystyle\Omega_{\textsc{Middle}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\{(\rho,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}:R_{1}+\varepsilon+f(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta,t)\}\leq\rho\leq R_{2}-\varepsilon+f(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta,t)\},

\displaystyle\Omega_{\textsc{Outer}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\{(\rho,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}:R_{2}+\varepsilon+f(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta,t)\}\leq\rho\leq r_{2}\}.

Remark 2.1
.

Here, we are considering
\varepsilon
small enough such that

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle<R_{1}-\varepsilon+f(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta,t),

\displaystyle R_{1}+\varepsilon+f(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta,t)

\displaystyle<R_{2}-\varepsilon+f(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta,t),

\displaystyle R_{2}+\varepsilon+f(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta,t)

\displaystyle<r_{2},

for all
t\geq 0
and
\theta\in\mathbb{T}.

Thus, in the remaining domain
\mathsf{C}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\setminus\Phi_{t}(D_{\varepsilon})
the scalar vorticity is just piecewise constant. Since we will define
\varpi
such that

\varpi(\rho)=2A+\begin{cases}0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in\Omega_{\textsc{Inner}}(t),\\
\varepsilon,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in\Omega_{\textsc{Middle}}(t),\\
0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in\Omega_{\textsc{Outer}}(t),\end{cases}

we have that

(13)

\omega(\rho(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),t)=2A+\begin{cases}0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in\Omega_{\textsc{Inner}}(t),\\
\varepsilon,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in\Omega_{\textsc{Middle}}(t),\\
0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in\Omega_{\textsc{Outer}}(t).\end{cases}

Remark 2.2
.

The precise definition of
\varpi
has been postponed to Section
2.3
. For this point, the only information we need to know is that

\displaystyle\text{supp}(\varpi)

\displaystyle=(R_{1}-\varepsilon,R_{2}+\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\text{supp}(\varpi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(R_{1}-\varepsilon,R_{1}+\varepsilon)\cup(R_{2}-\varepsilon,R_{2}+\varepsilon)\equiv I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}.

Using contour dynamics together with the equations (
12
) and (
13
) we obtain an equivalent formulation for the scalar vorticity equation (
10
)-(
11
) in terms of a time-evolution equation for
f(\cdot,t)
that depends only on the profile of
\varpi
, see (
15
).

Differentiating (
12
) with respect to
\theta
and with respect to
\rho
we have that

\displaystyle\nabla\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\nabla\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)\partial_{\rho}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=\varpi^{\prime}(\rho).

By straightforward computation, we get

(14)

\nabla\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)=\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(\rho)}{\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta)\cdot\partial_{\rho}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)}\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta).

Taking a time derivative in (
12
) and using (
10
) and (
14
) yields

\displaystyle 0=\frac{d}{dt}\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)+\nabla\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)\partial_{t}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=(-\nabla^{\perp}\psi(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)+\partial_{t}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta))\cdot\nabla\omega(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)

\displaystyle=(-\nabla^{\perp}\psi(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)+\partial_{t}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta))\cdot\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(\rho)}{\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta)\cdot\partial_{\rho}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)}\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta).

Since by definition of the profile function
\varpi
we have that
\text{supp}(\varpi^{\prime})=I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}
, the above expression reduces to

\partial_{t}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)\cdot\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta)=\nabla^{\perp}\psi(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)\cdot\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta).

Recalling that
\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)=(\rho+f(\rho,\theta,t))(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
and defining the auxiliary function
\bar{\psi}
adapted to the level sets

\bar{\psi}(\rho,\theta,t):=\psi(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t),\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},

we immediately get

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)\cdot\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=-(\rho+f(\rho,\theta,t))\partial_{t}f(\rho,\theta,t),

\displaystyle\nabla^{\perp}\psi(\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta),t)\cdot\partial_{\theta}\Phi_{t}^{\perp}(\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\bar{\psi}(\rho,\theta,t),

and consequently the problem reduces to solve the following equation

(15)

(\rho+f(\rho,\theta,t))\partial_{t}f(\rho,\theta,t)+\partial_{\theta}\bar{\psi}(\rho,\theta,t)=0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},\qquad t\geq 0,

with
\bar{\psi}=\psi\circ\Phi_{t}
and
\psi
solving (
11
).

2.1.1.
Recovering the solution of 2D Euler from the level sets

The above expression (
15
) gives us the equation satisfying the level sets
\Phi_{t}(\rho,\theta)=(\rho+f(\rho,\theta,t))(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
of the scalar vorticity of the 2D Euler equation (
10
) that we are interested in.

Conversely, if
f(\cdot,t)\in C^{\infty}(D_{\varepsilon})
with
\|f(t)\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\ll 1
for all
t\geq 0
satisfies the previous equation (
15
) on
D_{\varepsilon}
for some
\varpi\in C^{\infty}([r_{1},r_{2}])
, we can prove that the function
\omega(\cdot,t)
defined implicitly by
(
12
) on
D_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.
\omega=\varpi\circ\Phi_{t}^{-1}

and extended by suitable constants to the complementary of

\left\{{\bf x}\in\mathsf{C}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:{\bf x}=(\rho+f(\rho,\theta,t))(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\text{ with }(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon}\right\},

is a smooth solution of the original problem (
10
) over
\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
.

2.2.
The equation for the time periodic solution

In this manuscript we are interested in the existence of time periodic rotating solutions of (
15
). That is, we will look for solutions of the form

(16)

f(\rho,\theta,t):=\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta+\lambda t),\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},\quad t\geq 0,

for some value
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and some profile
\mathsf{f}\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon}).

Putting the previous ansatz (
16
) into (
15
), our original time-dependent problem reduces to solve the time-independent equation

(17)

\lambda(\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta))\partial_{\theta}\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta)+\partial_{\theta}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta)=0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},

with
\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta)=\psi(\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta),\theta)
and
\psi
solving (
11
).

Remark 2.3
.

It is important to emphasize that
\bar{\psi}=\psi\circ\Phi_{t}
depends functionally on the function
\mathsf{f}
. Then, we will write in the rest of the manuscript
\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]
to make that dependence explicit.

2.2.1.
The functional equation

Let us define our functional candidate taking into account (
15
). That is,

\tilde{F}[\lambda,\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta):=\lambda(\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta))\partial_{\theta}\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta)+\partial_{\theta}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta),\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},

which can be written also as

\tilde{F}[\lambda,\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta)=\partial_{\theta}[\lambda(\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta))^{2}/2+\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta)].

We also introduce the auxiliary functional

A[\lambda,\mathsf{f}](\rho):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\lambda(\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,s))^{2}/2+\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](\rho,s)\mbox{d}s,\qquad\rho\in I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}},

and define our final functional as

(18)

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta):=\lambda(\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta))^{2}/2+\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta)-A[\lambda,\mathsf{f}](\rho),\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon}.

Therefore, just by construction of
F[\cdot,\cdot]
, we have that
(\lambda,\mathsf{f})\in\mathbb{R}\times C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
satisfies

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\tilde{F}[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]=0.

Then, the problem of finding time periodic rotating solutions reduces to find the roots of the
nonlinear and nonlocal functional
F[\cdot,\cdot]
, that is, to study the equation

(19)

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}](\rho,\theta)=0,\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon}.

Remark 2.4
.

Note that a trivial curve of solutions is known

F[\lambda,0](\rho,\theta)=0,\quad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

The main goal of the paper is to show the existence of non-trivial solutions of the equation (
19
). We remind the reader that by non-trivial we mean solutions that depend on the angular variable in a non-trivial way. In particular, we prove the existence of time periodic rotating solutions of 2D Euler equation as close as we want to the steady Taylor-Couette bifurcating from the trivial one.

Remark 2.5
.

Notice that the solution given by

\omega((\rho+\mathsf{f}(\rho,\theta+\lambda t))(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),t)=\varpi(\rho),\qquad(\rho,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon},

and by expression
(
13
)
on the complementary set
give rise, by an appropriate change of variables, to solutions of 2D Euler equation (
2
)-(
4
) of the form

{\bf u}(r,\theta,t)=(u^{r}(r,\theta,t),u^{\theta}(r,\theta,t))=(\bar{u}^{r}(r,\theta+\lambda t),\bar{u}^{\theta}(r,\theta+\lambda t)).

2.3.
The profile function
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}

To continue, we define the profile function
\varpi\in C^{\infty}([r_{1},r_{2}])
that will be used in Section
6
to solve the functional equation (
19
).

To begin with, let us first consider a profile with compact support in a much less regular space, that is

\varpi\in W^{1,\infty}([r_{1},r_{2}])\cap H^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}^{-}}([r_{1},r_{2}]).

Let us consider the auxiliary function

\varphi(z)=\frac{1-z}{2},\quad|z|\leq 1.

Fixed
R_{1},R_{2}\in(r_{1},r_{2})
. Let
\varepsilon>0
, we define
\varpi_{\varepsilon}(r)
in the following way

\displaystyle\varpi_{\varepsilon}(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cr}0&r_{1}<r<R_{1}-\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon\varphi\left(\frac{R_{1}-r}{\varepsilon}\right)&R_{1}-\varepsilon\leq r\leq R_{1}+\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon&R_{1}+\varepsilon<r<R_{2}-\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon\varphi\left(\frac{r-R_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)&R_{2}-\varepsilon\leq r\leq R_{2}+\varepsilon,\\
0&R_{2}+\varepsilon<r<r_{2}.\end{array}\right.

Since we are interested in smooth solutions, we have to consider a smooth profile function of
\varpi_{\varepsilon}
.
In order to do that, we will use a
\kappa
-regularization of
\varphi
. The function
\varphi_{\kappa}:[-1,1]\mapsto[0,1]
will be defined by

(20)

\varphi_{\kappa}(z)=1-\frac{\int_{-1}^{z}\left(\int_{-1+\kappa}^{+1-\kappa}\Theta\left(\frac{\bar{z}-\bar{\bar{z}}}{\kappa}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{\bar{z}}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z}}{\int_{-1}^{+1}\left(\int_{-1+\kappa}^{+1-\kappa}\Theta\left(\frac{\bar{z}-\bar{\bar{z}}}{\kappa}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{\bar{z}}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z}},

where
\Theta
is a mollifier so that is smooth and positive with
\supp(\Theta)\subset(-1,1)
and
\int_{-1}^{+1}\Theta(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}=1.

We summarize the properties of this
\kappa
-regularization function in the following lemma without proof. For the interested reader, we will refer to
[
17
, Lemma 2.2]
for more details.

Lemma 2.6
.

The function
\varphi_{\kappa}
given by (
20
) satisfies

(1)

\varphi_{\kappa}(-1)=1
,
\varphi_{\kappa}(1)=0
,
(\partial_{z}^{n}\varphi_{\kappa})(\pm 1)=0
, for
n=1,2,\ldots

(2)

\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(z)<0
, for
|z|<1
.

(3)

\|\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}\|_{L^{1}([-1,1])}\leq C\kappa
.

The final profile that will be used in this manuscript is given by

\displaystyle\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cr}0&r_{1}<r<R_{1}-\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon\varphi_{\kappa}\left(\frac{R_{1}-r}{\varepsilon}\right)&R_{1}-\varepsilon\leq r\leq R_{1}+\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon&R_{1}+\varepsilon<r<R_{2}-\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon\varphi_{\kappa}\left(\frac{r-R_{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)&R_{2}-\varepsilon\leq r\leq R_{2}+\varepsilon,\\
0&R_{2}+\varepsilon<r<r_{2}.\end{array}\right.

Let us point out that
\supp(\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa})=I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}
. That is, the domain

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}=I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1}}\cup I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{2}}=(R_{1}-\varepsilon,R_{1}+\varepsilon)\cup(R_{2}-\varepsilon,R_{2}+\varepsilon)\subset[r_{1},r_{2}].

In principle the values of
R_{1},R_{2}
could be any real numbers just satisfying
r_{1}<R_{1}<R_{2}<r_{2}
. But as we will see in Section
6
to prove that
\text{dim}(\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}[\lambda_{\star}]))=1
we need to choose
R_{1},R_{2}
such that

u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(R_{1})\neq u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(R_{2}).

Moreover, the properties of the time periodic solution we construct depend strongly on these values. For example, the frequency of the rotation we obtain will be
u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(R_{1})
or
u_{\textsf{TC}}^{\theta}(R_{2}).

The rest of the manuscript consists of finding a non-trivial solution of (
19
) with
\varpi\equiv 2A+\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
in (
18
) for parameters
\varepsilon
and
\kappa
small enough. It will be done using the bifurcation theory through Crandall–Rabinowitz theorem
[
25
]
. For the completeness of the paper we recall this basic theorem and it will
referred to as sometimes by C-R theorem.

3.
Bifurcation theory and Crandall-Rabinowitz

The resolution of a nonlinear equation of the type (
19
) together with the existence of a trivial
line of solutions
F[\lambda,0]=0
can be studied through bifurcation theory. In particular, here we will apply
the well-known Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem whose proof can be found in
[
25
]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
X,Y
be two Banach spaces,
V
a neighborhood of
0
in
X
and let

F:\mathbb{R}\times V\to Y

with the following properties:

(1)

F[\lambda,0]=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

The partial derivatives
D_{\lambda}F
,
D_{\mathsf{f}}F
and
D^{2}_{\lambda,\mathsf{f}}F
exist and are continuous.

(3)

There exists
\lambda_{\star}
such that if
\mathcal{L}_{\star}=D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda_{\star},0]
then
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
and
Y/\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
are one-dimensional.

(4)

Transversality assumption:
D^{2}_{\lambda,\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda_{\star},0]\mathsf{h}_{\star}\not\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
, where

\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})=\text{span}\{\mathsf{h}_{\star}\}.

If
Z
is any complement of
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
in
X
, then there is a neighborhood
U
of
(\lambda_{\star},0)
in
\mathbb{R}\times X
, an interval
(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})
, and continuous functions
\varphi:(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})\to\mathbb{R}
,
\psi:(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})\to Z
such that
\varphi(0)=0
,
\psi(0)=0
and

\displaystyle F^{-1}(0)\cap U=

\displaystyle\Big\{\big(\lambda_{\star}+\varphi(\sigma),\sigma h_{\star}+\sigma\psi(\sigma)\big)\,;\,|\sigma|<\sigma_{0}\Big\}\cup\Big\{(\lambda,0)\,;\,(\lambda,0)\in U\Big\}.

The bulk of the work consists of proving all assumptions of Theorem
3.1
. This will be done in detail in the
the following sections.

3.1.
Functional setting and regularity

In order to apply the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem we need first to fix the functional spaces. We should look for Banach spaces
X
and
Y
such that
F:\mathbb{R}\times V\subset X\rightarrow Y
is well-defined and satisfies the required assumptions.

Our first step is to recall that we have to solve the functional equation
(
19
)
over
\overset{\circ}{\supp}(\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa})\times\mathbb{T}
, where the profile function
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
is given by (
2.3
). By definition, the support of
\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
is just

I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}=(R_{1}-\varepsilon,R_{1}+\varepsilon)\cup(R_{2}-\varepsilon,R_{2}+\varepsilon).

Then, recalling that
D_{\varepsilon}=I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}\times\mathbb{T}
, the spaces
X
and
Y
are given by

(26)

X(D_{\varepsilon}):=\left\{\mathsf{g}\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon}):\text{$\mathsf{g}$ is even in $\theta$ with}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathsf{g}(r,\theta)\mbox{d}\theta=0\right\},

and

(27)

Y(D_{\varepsilon}):=X(D_{\varepsilon}).

Here
C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
is the Hölder space of
2\pi
-periocic functions in the
\theta
-variable with norm

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{g}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}:=\|\mathsf{g}\|_{C^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}+|\nabla^{2}\mathsf{g}|_{C^{0,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},

where
\|\cdot\|_{C^{2}}
and
|\cdot|_{C^{0,\alpha}}
are the standard norm and semi-norm respectively.

Given these definitions, the main objective of this section is to show the following lemmas:

Lemma 3.2
.

For all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(r_{1},R_{1},R_{2},r_{2}),
there exist
\delta(\varepsilon_{0})
small enough such that

\displaystyle F\,:\,\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))

\displaystyle\to Y(D_{\varepsilon}),

\displaystyle\quad(\lambda,\mathsf{f})

\displaystyle\to F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon})):=\{\mathsf{g}\in X(D_{\varepsilon})\,:\,\|\mathsf{g}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}<\delta\}.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{f}\in X(D_{\varepsilon})
, we have that
F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\in Y(D_{\varepsilon})
by direct application of the next three facts:

i)

\mathsf{f}\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon}).

ii)

\mathsf{f}\text{ is even in }\theta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\text{ is even in }\theta.

iii)

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]
is mean zero in the angular variable.

Proof of i).
Recall that
\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]=\psi[\mathsf{f}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}]
with
\Phi[\mathsf{f}]=(\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}],\Phi_{2}[\mathsf{f}])=(r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon}).
Since
r\in I_{\varepsilon}^{R_{1},R_{2}}\subset[r_{1},r_{2}]
with
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty
and the norm
\left\lVert\mathsf{f}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}
is small, we can define their inverse
\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]=(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}],\Phi_{2}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Phi[\mathsf{f}](D_{\varepsilon}))
, with
\Phi[\mathsf{f}](D_{\varepsilon})\subsetneq\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
satisfying

r_{1}<\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{1}-\varepsilon),\quad\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{1}+\varepsilon)<\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{2}-\varepsilon),\quad\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{2}+\varepsilon)<r_{2}.

As
\varpi\in C^{\infty}([r_{1},r_{2}])
and
\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Phi[\mathsf{f}](D_{\varepsilon}))
, we have that
\omega[\mathsf{f}]
given by (
31
) belongs to
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})
. Moreover, applying elliptic regularity theory into the system (
30
) we obtain that

\psi[\mathsf{f}]\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}).

Therefore, we can conclude that
\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]=\psi[\mathsf{f}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}]\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon}).

Proof of ii).
Since
\mathsf{f}
is even in the angular variable, the result follows immediately by the definition of
\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta)=\psi[\mathsf{f}](r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)
and the evenness of
\psi[\mathsf{f}]
in the angular variable. To obtain the evenness of
\psi[\mathsf{f}]
we just remember that it is solution of the elliptic problem
\Delta_{B}\psi[\mathsf{f}]=\omega[\mathsf{f}]
with an even “source” term. The latter is deduced by the facts that
\omega[\mathsf{f}](r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)=\varpi(r)
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
, and
\mathsf{f}
is even in the angular variable by hypothesis.

Proof of iii).
This is direct consequence of the definition of the auxiliary functional
A[\lambda,\mathsf{f}].

∎

Moreover, the functional satisfies the regularity conditions given by the next lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

For all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(r_{1},R_{1},R_{2},r_{2}),
there exist
\delta(\varepsilon_{0})
small enough such that

(1)

The functional
F:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))\rightarrow Y(D_{\varepsilon})
is of class
C^{1}.

(2)

The partial derivative
D^{2}_{\lambda,\mathsf{f}}F:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))\rightarrow Y(D_{\varepsilon})
is continuous.

Proof.

The derivatives with respect
\lambda
are trivial. Consequently, the result reduces to proving that there exists the Fréchet differential of
F
:

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\,:\,X(D_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\to Y(D_{\varepsilon}),

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{h}

\displaystyle\to D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}

and that it is continuous for all
\mathsf{f}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

This will be done by firstly showing the existence of the linear Gâteaux derivative and secondly its continuity in the strong topology.

Actually, the Gâteaux differential of
F[\lambda,\cdot]
at
\mathsf{f}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
in the direction
\mathsf{h}\in X(D_{\varepsilon})

is defined as

D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}:=\frac{\mbox{d}}{{\mbox{d}\tau}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]|_{\tau=0}=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}.

A formal straightforward computation show that this derivative is given by

(28)

D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r,\theta)=\lambda(r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta))\mathsf{h}(r,\theta)+D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r,\theta)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\lambda(r+\mathsf{f}(r,s))\mathsf{h}(r,s)+D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r,s)\,\mbox{d}s.

To prove this rigorously, we need to get

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\frac{F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

By virtue of (
28
), and straightforward computations, it is enough to prove that there exists
D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
such that

(29)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\frac{\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

In order to prove that, we start recalling that for a generic function
f\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
we have that
\bar{\psi}[f](\rho,\theta)=\psi[f](\Phi[f])(r,\theta)=\psi[f](\rho+f(\rho,\theta),\theta)
with
\psi[f]
solving the problem

(30)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{B}\psi[f](r,\theta)&=\omega[f](r,\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},\\
\psi[f](r_{1},\theta)&=0,\\
\psi[f](r_{2},\theta)&=\gamma,\end{aligned}\right.

and

(31)

\omega[f](r,\theta)=2A+\begin{cases}0\qquad&r_{1}\leq r<\Phi_{1}[f](R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[f](r,\theta))\qquad&\Phi_{1}[f](R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq\Phi_{1}[f](R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\varepsilon&\Phi_{1}[f](R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta)<r<\Phi_{1}[f](R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[f](r,\theta))\qquad&\Phi_{1}[f](R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq\Phi_{1}[f](R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\Phi_{1}[f](R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta)<r\leq r_{2},\end{cases}

where

\Phi[f](r,\theta)\equiv(\Phi_{1}[f](r,\theta),\Phi_{2}[f](r,\theta)):=(r+f(r,\theta),\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon}.

Remark 3.4
.

In this section, we remove the subscripts
\varepsilon
and
\kappa
from
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
to alleviate
the notation.

Proceeding similarly as before, we start computing formally
D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
, which can be compute as

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\tau}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\bigg|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\psi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d}{d\tau}\psi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]+\left(\nabla\psi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\cdot\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\bigg|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}]+\left(\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}]\right)\mathsf{h}.

That is,

(32)

D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r,\theta):=D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)+\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}](r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)\mathsf{h}(r,\theta).

Since the second term of the above expression (
32
) is just a derivative in the classical sense and
\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}](r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)\mathsf{h}(r,\theta)\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
(remember that
\psi\in C^{4,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})
), we have that (
29
) reduces to prove that there exists
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
such that

(33)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\frac{\psi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

Taking into account (
30
) we have that
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
should solve the problem

(34)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{B}\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r,\theta)&=D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r,\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},\\
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r_{1},\theta)&=0,\\
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r_{2},\theta)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Now, we are going to obtain an explicit expression for the “source” term of (
34
). That is, we are going to compute the differential of
\omega[f]
with respect
f
at the point
\mathsf{f}
in the direction
\mathsf{h}
.

Recalling (
31
), that is
\omega[f](\Phi[f](r,\theta))=\varpi(r)
for
(r,\theta)\in D_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)+\partial_{r}\omega[\mathsf{f}](r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)\mathsf{h}(r,\theta)=0,

and

\partial_{r}\omega[\mathsf{f}](r+\mathsf{f}(r,\theta),\theta)(1+\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}(r,\theta))=\varpi^{\prime}(r)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\partial_{r}\omega[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta)=\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))}{1+\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))}.

Hence, we immediately get the candidate

(35)

D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}(r,\theta)=\begin{cases}0\qquad&r_{1}\leq r<\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
-\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))\mathsf{h}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))}{1+\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))}\qquad&\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta)<r<\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
-\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))\mathsf{h}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))}{1+\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta))}\qquad&\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta)<r\leq r_{2}.\end{cases}

At this point, combining (
30
) and (
34
) we obtain that auxiliary function

\Psi_{\tau}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}:=D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\psi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau},

solves (by definition) the elliptic-type problem

(36)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{B}\left(\Psi_{\tau}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r,\theta)&=\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right)(r,\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},\\
\left(\Psi_{\tau}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r_{1},\theta)&=0,\\
\left(\Psi_{\tau}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r_{2},\theta)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Hence, by classical elliptic regularity theory we can conclude from (
36
) that

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\psi[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\lesssim\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}.

with
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
given by (
35
). Now, we will check that actually

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}=0,

what it implies (
33
). In order to do that, we start analyzing in detail the difference
\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]
.
Without lost of generality, the direction
\mathsf{h}\in X(D_{\varepsilon})
can be taken with norm
\left\lVert\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}=1
. Then, taking
\tau
small enough, we have that

(37)

\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]=\begin{cases}0&r_{1}\leq r<\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]&\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\quad&\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]&\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]&\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\quad&\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]&\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq r_{2},\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(r,\theta):

\displaystyle=\min\{\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta),\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta)\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(r,\theta):

\displaystyle=\max\{\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta),\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](r,\theta)\}.

Remark 3.5
.

Over each region
\{(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)\}
with
i,j=1,2
we have that the difference
\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]
will be

\varpi(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}])\qquad\text{or}\qquad\varpi(\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]).

In addition, we define the auxiliary sets

(38)

V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon):=\{(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)<r<\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)\},

and

(39)

U_{\tau}^{k}:=\{(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:\mathsf{M}_{\tau}(R_{k}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq\mathsf{m}_{\tau}(R_{k}+\varepsilon,\theta)\}.

Then, as an immediate consequence of (
35
)-(
37
) and (
38
)-(
39
) we have that

D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}=0,\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i,j=1,2}V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon)\cup\bigcup_{k=1,2}U_{\tau}^{k}\right).

Hence, we can directly write

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}\\
+\sum_{k=1,2}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})}.

Now, we are going to study each of the terms of the previous expression. On the one hand, applying (
35
) and (
37
) we obtain

(40)

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})}\\
=\left\lVert\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\right)}-\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]-\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})}.

Here we will give all the details on how to manage the above type of term, which will appear repeatedly in this section. First of all, from Taylor’s expansion, we can ensure that there exists
\tau^{\ast}\in(0,\tau)
such that

\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]=\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\big|_{\tau=0}+\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=0}\tau\\
+\frac{d^{2}}{d^{2}\tau}\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}\frac{\tau^{2}}{2},

with

(41)

\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=0}=\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\cdot\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\bigg|_{\tau=0},

and

\frac{d^{2}}{d^{2}\tau}\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}=\varpi^{\prime\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\cdot\left(\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)^{2}\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}\\
+\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\cdot\frac{d^{2}}{d^{2}\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}.

Since
\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta))=r
and
\Phi_{2}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta))=\theta
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta)+\mathsf{f}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta))+\tau\mathsf{h}(\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta))=r,

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](r,\theta)=\theta,

and calculating their derivatives with respect to
\tau
-variable, we obtain

(42)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{1+(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}])+\tau(\partial_{r}\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}])},

(43)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{2}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]

\displaystyle=0,

and proceeding similarly

(44)

\left[1+(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}])+\tau(\partial_{r}\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}])\right]\frac{d^{2}}{d^{2}\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\\
+\left[\left(\partial_{r}^{2}\mathsf{f}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)+\tau\left(\partial_{r}^{2}\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\right]\left(\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)^{2}\\
+2\partial_{r}\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\,\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]=0.

Then, combining the above, we have that (
40
) is just

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})}=\frac{\tau}{2}\left\lVert\frac{d^{2}}{d^{2}\tau}\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})},

which can be upper bounded, taking
\tau
small enough and using (
42
)-(
44
), as follows

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})}\leq C(\|\mathsf{f}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},\|\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},\|\varpi\|_{C^{2,\alpha}([r_{1},r_{2}])})\tau.

In particular, we have proved that

(45)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{\tau}^{k})}=0.

On the other hand, since
|V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon)|=O(\tau)
and we have computed explicitly (
35
) and (
37
) we obtain that

(46)

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}\\
+\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-\frac{(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])}{1+(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{-}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))},

where we have used the disjoint union given by

V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))=V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))\cup V_{\tau}^{-}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon)),

with

\displaystyle V_{\tau}^{+}(z):=\{(r,\theta)\in V_{\tau}(z):\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](z,\theta)<r<\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](z,\theta)\},

\displaystyle V_{\tau}^{-}(z):=\{(r,\theta)\in V_{\tau}(z):\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}](z,\theta)<r<\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}](z,\theta)\},

for
z\in\left\{R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon:i,j=1,2\right\}
.

Since
\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\equiv 0
over
V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon)
by definition, we directly have that

\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}.

Moreover, applying Taylor’s expansion as we did before, we can ensure that there exists
\tau^{\ast}\in(0,\tau)
such that

\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=\left\lVert\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\right)\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}.

In fact, using (
41
) and the fact that
C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))
is a Banach algebra we get

\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}\leq\left\lVert\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau^{\ast}\mathsf{h}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}\\
\left\lVert\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))},

with (see (
42
))

\left\lVert\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]\bigg|_{\tau=\tau^{\ast}}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}\lesssim\frac{\left\lVert\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}}{1-\left(\left\lVert\mathsf{f}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}+\tau^{\ast}\left\lVert\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\right)},

and

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau^{\ast}\mathsf{h}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=0,

where, in the limit above, we have used the fact that
\varpi
is smooth with
\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]\equiv 0
over
V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon)
and
\tau^{\ast}\in(0,\tau).

Hence, we have concluded that

(47)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{+}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=0,

and proceeding in a similar way, using the same type of arguments, we can also conclude that

(48)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert\frac{\varpi\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-\frac{(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])}{1+(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}])}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}^{-}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=0.

Applying (
47
)-(
48
) into (
46
) we finally get our desired goal. That is,

(49)

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{\tau}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}=0.

To sum up, combining (
49
) and (
45
) we have proved that

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}-\frac{\omega[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}=0,

and consequently, going backwards in our argument, we have proven our initial objective (
29
), i.e.,

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\frac{\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0,

or equivalently,

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\frac{F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau\mathsf{h}]-F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}\right\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

This shows the existence of Gâteaux derivative and now we intend to prove the continuity of the map
\mathsf{f}\to D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]

from
B_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
to the space
\mathsf{L}(X(D_{\varepsilon}),Y(D_{\varepsilon}))
of bounded linear operators from
X(D_{\varepsilon})
to
Y(D_{\varepsilon})
. This is a consequence of the following estimate:

(50)

\|D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},

for any pair
\mathsf{f}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
and
\mathsf{h}\in X(D_{\varepsilon}).

By virtue of (
28
) we get

D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}=\lambda(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})\mathsf{h}-\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\lambda(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})\mathsf{h}-\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)\,\mbox{d}s,

and as a direct consequence we obtain that to prove (
50
) it is enough to verify that

(51)

\|D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

Furthermore, taking into account the explicit expression of
D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
given by (
32
) and by adding and subtracting appropriate terms we can write

D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}=D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\pm D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\\
+\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\pm\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}].

Next, we have to study each of the following terms

(52)

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},\quad\left\lVert\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},

and

(53)

\left\lVert\left(\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},\quad\|\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

Let us start with (
52
), we recall that
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h},\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\in C^{3,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})
. Then, taking
\mathsf{g}
equal to
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}
or
\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]
we get

\mathsf{g}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}](r,\theta)-\mathsf{g}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}](r,\theta)=\mathsf{g}(r+\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(r,\theta),\theta)-\mathsf{g}(r+\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}(r,\theta),\theta)\\
=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{s}\left[\mathsf{g}(r+s\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(r,\theta)+(1-s)\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}(r,\theta),\theta)\right]\,\mbox{d}s\\
=\left(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(r,\theta)-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}(r,\theta)\right)\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{r}\mathsf{g}(r+s\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(r,\theta)+(1-s)\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}(r,\theta),\theta)\,\mbox{d}s.

Since
\mathsf{g}\in C^{3,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})
and
\mathsf{f}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
we directly have that

\partial_{r}\mathsf{g}(r+s\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(r,\theta)+(1-s)\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}(r,\theta),\theta)\in C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon}),\qquad\forall s\in(0,1),

and consequently, using the fact that
C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})
is Banach algebra, we can conclude that

\|\mathsf{g}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\mathsf{g}\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

The first factor of (
53
) can be handled as follows

\left\lVert\left(\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\circ\Phi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\leq\left\lVert\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\\
\leq\left\lVert\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{3,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\lesssim\left\lVert\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})},

where, in the last step, we have used the fact that
\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]
solves the elliptic-type problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{B}\left(\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)(r,\theta)&=\left(\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)(r,\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},\\
\left(\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)(r_{1},\theta)&=0,\\
\left(\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)(r_{2},\theta)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Proceeding in the same way as we did before we get
\left\lVert\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\leq\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

Then, combining all the above we have proved that

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}+\|D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})},

and we conclude that (
51
) reduces to check that

(54)

\|D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

Since
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}
and
D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}
solve an elliptic-type problem like (
34
), we have that their difference satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta_{B}\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r,\theta)&=\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r,\theta),\qquad(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}},\\
\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r_{1},\theta)&=0,\\
\left(D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right)(r_{2},\theta)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Applying classical elliptic regularity theory we have

(55)

\|D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\psi[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\lesssim\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}.

Therefore, combining (
54
) and (
55
), our original problem is reduced to prove

(56)

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

Since we have an explicit expression for
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}]\mathsf{h}
given by (
35
). We start analyzing in detail their difference. That is,

D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}=\\
\begin{cases}0\qquad&r_{1}\leq r<\mathrm{m}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\quad&\mathrm{m}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{M}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}-\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}\quad&\mathrm{M}(R_{1}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{m}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\quad&\mathrm{m}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{M}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\mathrm{M}(R_{1}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{m}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\quad&\mathrm{m}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{M}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta),\\
\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}-\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}\qquad&\mathrm{M}(R_{2}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{m}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\quad&\mathrm{m}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{M}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta),\\
0&\mathrm{M}(R_{2}+\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq r_{2}\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{m}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle:=\min\{\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}](r,\theta),\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}](r,\theta)\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle:=\max\{\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}](r,\theta),\Phi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}](r,\theta)\}.

Remark 3.6
.

Over each region
\{(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:\mathrm{m}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r<\mathrm{M}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)\}
with
i,j=1,2
we have that the difference
D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\omega[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}
will be

-\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}\qquad\text{or}\qquad-\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}.

We can proceed as we did before and define the auxiliary sets

V(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon):=\{(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:\mathrm{m}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)<r<\mathrm{M}(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon,\theta)\},

and

U^{k}:=\{(r,\theta)\in\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:\mathrm{M}(R_{k}-\varepsilon,\theta)\leq r\leq\mathrm{m}(R_{k}+\varepsilon,\theta)\}.

Then, as an immediate consequence we get

(57)

\|D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathsf{P}_{r_{1},r_{2}})}=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(V(R_{i}+(-1)^{j}\varepsilon))}\\
+\sum_{k=1,2}\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}.

Now, we are going to study each of the terms of the previous expression. On the one hand, we have

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}=\left\lVert\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}-\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})},

which can be decomposed and upper bounded (adding and subtracting appropriate terms) as follows

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\\
\leq\left\lVert\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left[\frac{1}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}-\frac{1}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}\right]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\\
+\left\lVert\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left[\frac{1}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)}-\frac{1}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}\right]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\\
+\left\lVert\frac{\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right)-\left(\varpi^{\prime}\circ\Phi_{1}^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)\left(\mathsf{h}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}{1+\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right)}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}.

Since Hölder space
C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})
is a Banach algebra we get

\left\lVert D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\mathsf{h}-D_{\mathsf{f}}\bar{\psi}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\leq\frac{1}{1-\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}}\left\lVert(\varpi^{\prime}\mathsf{h})\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]-(\varpi^{\prime}\mathsf{h})\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\\
+\frac{\left\lVert\varpi\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}([r_{1},r_{2}])}\left\lVert\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}}{1-\max\{\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})},\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\}}\left\lVert\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\right)\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\\
+\frac{\left\lVert\varpi\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}([r_{1},r_{2}])}\left\lVert\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}}{1-\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}}\left\lVert\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}.

In addition, we have directly that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\varpi^{\prime}\mathsf{h})\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]-(\varpi^{\prime}\mathsf{h})\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\varpi^{\prime}\mathsf{h}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\left\lVert\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]-\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}\left\lVert\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]-\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\partial_{r}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\right)\circ\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

To conclude, we only need to see that

(58)

\left\lVert\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]-\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(U^{k})}\leq\left\lVert\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

In order to prove this we apply once again Taylor’s expansion, which ensures that there exists
\tau^{\ast}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]-\Phi^{-1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]
